## Page 1


A SINGULAR STOCHASTIC WAVE–KLEIN–GORDON SYSTEM WITH
ROUGH GAUSSIAN FORCING AND DISTINCT PROPAGATION
SPEEDS
GUANGQIAN ZHAO
Abstract. We construct spatially local paracontrolled solutions on R3 for the stochastic
wave–Klein–Gordon system
(∂2
t −c2
W∆)u = uv + ξW,
(∂2
t −c2
K∆+ m2)v = uv + ξK,
with cW ̸= cK. The independent forcings are white in time and stationary in space, generated
by Fourier multipliers of respective orders βW, βK, and we assume β∗:= max{βW, βK} < 1/8.
The main analytic step is the construction of the mixed random operators Ia(w ≺Ψb)◦Ψc.
At finite cutoff they decompose into a centered second Gaussian chaos and, when b = c, a
deterministic covariance contraction. In the latter case the two propagation channels are
different; the resulting low–high phase gap gives the diagonal shell bound N −1+2βb. The
centered terms are controlled through four Schatten flattenings of a continuous-frequency
kernel with two physical localizers. Weighted phase-layer estimates also construct the first
Picard and cubic stochastic terms.
A localized auxiliary system, a finite-cutoff Sobolev bootstrap, and finite propagation
yield a compatible family of local solutions with compact-dependent measurable lifetimes.
We prove uniqueness in the stated paracontrolled class, local Lipschitz dependence on the
enhanced data and Cauchy data, independence of the localization pair, and convergence of
the spectral approximations and nonlinear sources. Fixed cutoff profiles converge almost
surely along the full sequence; arbitrary admissible cofinal cutoffs converge in probability up
to the reference lifetime.

A Volterra integration by parts supplies one inverse power of the high frequency. Since the
color-b covariance contributes |hb(ℓ)|2 ≲N2βb on |ℓ| ∼N, the resulting diagonal shell has size
N−1+2βb. If b ̸= c, independence of the colors eliminates the contraction and leaves a centered
second chaos. Multiplication by the inner physical localizer shifts the Duhamel frequency
from q + ℓto q + ℓ+ z; retaining its output shell separates the principal transfer region from
a Schwartz-decaying remainder.

Both cubic components are selected by the finite-cutoff Wick algebra. For a triple (a; b, c), the
centered operator carries the order βb + βc ≤2β∗. The restriction β∗< 1/8 leaves a nonempty

and operators.

The main results are stated in Theorems 2.33 and 2.34. The first gives local existence,
uniqueness among the solutions of Definition 2.30 for fixed (Θ, Ξ), independence of the
localization, and local Lipschitz stability. For each fixed cutoff profile, the second gives
almost-sure full-sequence dyadic convergence on a profile-dependent probability-one event.
For an arbitrary admissible cofinal sequence, the finite-cutoff solutions are defined only on
Borel existence events, and convergence in probability includes convergence of those events
below the reference lifetime.
Scope of the result. The construction is spatially local: every compact set K has an almost
surely positive measurable lifetime τK, and the argument does not require a common positive
lifetime over all of R3. Uniqueness is formulated for fixed enhanced data (Θ, Ξ) in the class of
Definition 2.30. For a fixed cutoff profile the full dyadic sequence converges almost surely on
a profile-dependent event, whereas general admissible cofinal cutoffs converge in probability
in the sense of Definition 2.29. The threshold β∗< 1/8 is sufficient for the estimates below;
optimality and a stopping-time formulation of the lifetimes are not considered here.
Relation to earlier work. Hairer’s theory of regularity structures [9] provides a general
local-expansion framework for singular SPDEs. For the three-dimensional quadratic stochastic
wave equation, the paracontrolled approach was developed by Gubinelli, Koch, and Oh [8].
The operator-valued Gaussian estimates used below are related to the random tensor estimates
of Deng, Nahmod, and Yue [4]; see also Kaneshiro [12] for a new proof of the abstract random
tensor estimate.
Related periodic and full-space constructions appear in the author’s preprints [17, 18]. The
present paper treats a massless–massive wave–Klein–Gordon system on R3 and combines
continuous-frequency stochastic estimates with physical localization, finite propagation, and a
local paracontrolled well-posedness theory.

Section 2 introduces the localization, approximation classes, function spaces, and main the-
orems. Section 3 derives the paracontrolled system and constructs the stochastic distributions
and operators entering it. The operators T a;b,c are constructed in Section 4. Section 5 contains
the deterministic fixed point, measurable lifetimes, spectral approximation, and localization
argument. The appendices prove the first Picard estimates and the estimates for Γa.

Remark 2.35 (Scope of the main theorems). The family in Theorem 2.33 is local in space,
with a measurable almost surely positive lifetime τK for each compact K. Uniqueness is
understood for fixed enhanced data (Θ, Ξ) in the class of Definition 2.30. In Theorem 2.34,
the almost-sure statement is tied to a fixed cutoff profile, while general cofinal cutoffs are
treated through the partially defined random variables of Definition 2.29. The proof uses only
the measurability of τK, and the sufficient threshold β∗< 1/8 is not claimed to be optimal.
Remark 2.36 (Reference realization). The pathwise construction starts from one smooth cutoff
profile. Its dyadic approximations give measurable realizations of the stochastic data and the
solution, and the cutoff-tail estimates identify their limits with the profile-independent objects.
Theorem 2.34(ii) shows that every admissible cofinal sequence converges to the same solution.
For a fixed profile, full-sequence convergence follows from the finite-state stabilization of
dyadic shells in Lemma 4.21.

4. Resonant operators
The proof is organized in three layers. First, Wick’s formula is applied to the two Gaussian
legs before any deterministic estimate: equal colors produce a covariance contraction and
all remaining terms form a completely centered second chaos. Second, a model-independent
two-step Gaussian Khintchine argument bounds a centered operator by four oriented Schatten
flattenings. Third, the localized wave–Klein–Gordon block is written with both physical
multipliers and the Duhamel frequency seen after localization. The resulting kernel identifies
the four Hilbert-space directions, supplies the N−1 propagator gain on the principal transfer
sector, and turns the remaining transfers into Schwartz tails. The Wick algebra determines
which branch occurs, while the frequency kernel determines its size.
4.1. Finite-cutoff decomposition. The four labels are obtained from (3.11). If (a; b, c) ∈
Ldiag, then b = c and a = b⊥; the covariance contraction therefore compares the dispersions
ωa and ωb. If (a; b, c) ∈Loff, then a = b and b ̸= c; independence of the two colors eliminates
the covariance contraction.
Wick’s identity is applied to the two stochastic Fourier variables before any deterministic
kernel estimate is made. The physical multipliers do not alter the covariance support r = −ℓ;
they only introduce two deterministic Schwartz convolution variables. The full two-localizer
kernel, including the frequency actually seen by the Duhamel propagator, is retained below.
Fix an inner stochastic scale N, a low input scale Q ≤capN, a Duhamel-output scale R, an
outer stochastic scale L, a final output scale M, and times 0 ≤s ≤t ≤T. The outer resonant
product imposes L ∼ap R. Before the two physical multipliers in (3.10) are inserted, every
translation-invariant dyadic core is a finite sum of operators of the form

4.3. Centered operators and the operator estimate. We begin with the Hilbert-space es-
timate for a centered quadratic Gaussian operator. The self-adjoint Gaussian noncommutative
Khintchine inequality is used in its standard form; Hermitian dilation gives the rectangular
estimate, and Gaussian polarization gives the order-two decoupling identity. The subsequent
lemmas incorporate covariance synthesis, continuous-frequency localization, time regularity,
and dyadic Sobolev–Besov summation.
4.3.1. Gaussian operator estimate from four Schatten flattenings. All Hilbert spaces in this
subsection are real. Complex Fourier L2 spaces are understood through realification, which
changes none of the operator or Schatten norms. Let A, C1, C0, E be separable Hilbert spaces,
and let H be a finite-rank four-linear form on A × C1 × C0 × E. Its four oriented flattenings
are defined by
⟨F1(x ⊗y ⊗z), w⟩= H(x, y, z, w),
F1 : A ⊗2 C1 ⊗2 C0 →E,

Lemma 4.10 (Uniform compression on compact Schatten sets). Let 2 ≤r < ∞, let Pm and
Qm be finite-rank orthogonal projections on separable Hilbert spaces H0 and H1, respectively,
and assume that Pm →IH0 and Qm →IH1 strongly. If K ⊂Sr(H0, H1) is compact, then
sup

(4.34)
The same conclusion holds for tensor products of strongly convergent finite-rank projections

Proof. For a fixed T ∈Sr, approximate T in Sr by a finite-rank map T (0). Strong convergence
is uniform on the finite-dimensional range and co-range of T (0), so
∥(I −Qm)T (0)∥Sr + ∥T (0)(I −Pm)∥Sr −→0.

Contractivity of orthogonal projections in every Schatten norm then gives the same conclusion
for T. To make the convergence uniform on K, choose a finite ε-net T1, . . . , TJ in Sr. For T
within ε of Tj, the two compression errors for T −Tj are at most 2ε each, while the errors for
the finite list (Tj) tend to zero uniformly in j. Letting ε ↓0 proves (4.34). Strong convergence
of tensor-product projections follows first on elementary tensors and then by density and
uniform boundedness. A finite union of compact sets is compact, which proves the final
assertion.
□

limit. The Hermitian realization in (4.56) identifies the two opposite-frequency coordinates

Consequently the restriction of mΛ to a fixed dyadic shell has only k+ −k−+ 2 possible states
as Λ ranges over the dyadic cutoffs. The inhomogeneous unit shell is handled separately and
also has only finitely many states. More generally, for any fixed number of cutoff factors
carried by arbitrary dyadic shells (not necessarily comparable), their joint restriction has a
scale-independent finite number of states. This number depends only on the cutoff profile, the
shell support constants, and the number of factors, not on the ratios of the shell scales.
In particular, if a dyadic random block XΛ,N,Q,M depends on Λ only through these cutoff
factors and every one of its possible states satisfies ∥∥X∥∥Lp ≤AN,Q,M, then





 sup
Λ∈2N0
∥XΛ,N,Q,M∥





Lp
≲m AN,Q,M.
(4.65)
For every fixed block, XΛ,N,Q,M agrees with its uncut value for all sufficiently large Λ.
Proof. If κ+N ≤c0Λ, the plateau condition gives mΛ = 1 on the shell. If κ−N > C0Λ, the
support condition gives mΛ = 0 there. Since N and Λ are dyadic, only finitely many integer
values of log2(Λ/N) lie between these two regimes. For a fixed collection of J shells, order
their logarithmic scales. Each factor can change only on an interval of uniformly bounded
integer length around its own scale; outside that interval it is identically zero or identically
one. As log2 Λ increases, the joint vector of restrictions can therefore change only on the union
of J such intervals and at their endpoints. It has at most CmJ + 1 states, independently of
all ratios between the shells. The maximal estimate follows by bounding the supremum by
the sum over this finite state set and using the triangle inequality in Lp. The plateau regime
gives stabilization.
□

For distinct colors, the scalar pairing of the centered operator with (f, g) is the ordered double
Wiener integral of kf,g. For equal colors, it is the second Wiener integral of the symmetrization

this scalar integral. If, in addition, all four flattenings of H♯lie in Sr for some 2 ≤r < ∞,
this scalar limit agrees with the pairing of the operator-valued completion in Theorem 4.15.

Wiener integral. The auxiliary plateaux act as the identity on the ranges of the synthesis maps,
so the coefficient has the multiplier in (4.87); for equal colors, the covariance term is (4.88).

The limit is independent of the admissible cutoff family. Along a fixed-profile dyadic family,
convergence holds almost surely in the same operator topology. The moment bounds are

Proof. Choose η > 0 below the strict margins in Lemma 4.33. Apply Proposition 4.29 and
Minkowski’s inequality. This gives a uniform Lp bound for the assembled operators in (4.130).
For two arbitrary admissible cofinal cutoffs, first choose a finite dyadic set whose complement
is uniformly small by the assembly lemma. On the finite set, fixed-block convergence from
Proposition 4.29 gives convergence in Lp operator norm. This proves the Cauchy property
and also shows that the limit is independent of the cutoff profile; no nesting of the multipliers
is required. Proposition 4.36 shows in addition that spectral removal, Gaussian finite-rank
completion, and dyadic assembly have a joint limit, so the definition of the centered operator
does not depend on an implicit order of approximation.
For a fixed-profile dyadic family, apply Lemma 4.35 and repeat the deterministic assembly
pathwise. This gives full-sequence almost-sure convergence on one probability-one event.
Strong measurability was established in the assembly lemma. The constants in the centered
proof use the individual wave and Klein–Gordon propagator bounds, the fixed localizers,
the dyadic support constants, and the shellwise bounds for the two forcing profiles. Hence
they are uniform on separated speed–mass classes, on compact subsets of the admissible
(βW, βK) region, and on sets with bounded forcing-profile Hprof. The centered branch uses
only single-channel propagator bounds.
□

the same conclusions for the complementary sectors, with absolutely summable Schwartz

the triangle inequality; the common fixed-profile event is the finite intersection of the principal
and remainder events.

using the little-Besov convention following (2.53). Separability follows from the same comple-
tion convention. At a fixed finite set of spatial shells, all energy and Strichartz components
take values in separable Sobolev or Lebesgue spaces, and the corresponding continuous- or
finite-p time path spaces are separable. On those finite shells the two L∞
T B2,∞seminorms
are equivalent to finite maxima of the CT L2 block norms already supplied by the energy
components, so no nonseparable ambient L∞space is introduced. Taking rational linear
combinations from countable dense subsets and then the countable union over finite shell sets
gives a countable dense family in each of these completed spaces. Finite products preserve
separability.

Lemma 5.7 (Measurable contraction selection). Let (Ω, F) be a measurable space, let B be a
separable Banach space, let G ∈F, and let R > 0. Suppose
Φ : Ω× BR −→B
is jointly Borel measurable and that there is a deterministic 0 ≤λ < 1 such that, for every
ω ∈G, the map z 7→Φ(ω, z) sends BR into itself and is λ-Lipschitz. Then the unique fixed


## Page 65


A STOCHASTIC WAVE–KLEIN–GORDON SYSTEM WITH ROUGH FORCING
65
point z∗(ω) on G, extended by 0 on Gc, is strongly measurable as a B-valued map. On G it is
the pointwise and norm limit of the measurable Picard iterates
z0 = 0,
zm+1(ω) = 1G(ω)Φ(ω, zm(ω)).
Proof. Extend the iteration by setting zm = 0 on Gc at every stage. Joint Borel measurability
of Φ and measurability of G show inductively that every zm is Borel measurable. On G, the
contraction estimate gives
∥zm+1 −zm∥B ≤λm∥z1 −z0∥B ≤2Rλm,
so the iterates converge in B to the unique fixed point. A pointwise norm limit of Borel maps
into a separable Banach space is strongly measurable.
□
Corollary 5.8 (Measurability of the paracontrolled fixed point). Fix T and a deterministic
radius R. Let G be a Borel event on which the random map PΞ(ω),y is a self-map and a
contraction of the closed radius-R ball in ZT , with a deterministic contraction constant strictly
smaller than one. Choose the strongly measurable realizations of the stochastic paths and
operators constructed above. Then the fixed point on G, extended by 0 on Gc, is a strongly
measurable ZT -valued random variable.
Proof. By Lemma 5.6, ZT is separable. For each fixed Z, every term in PΞ(ω),yZ is obtained
by evaluating a strongly measurable stochastic path or operator component and then applying
a deterministic continuous multilinear map; it is therefore strongly measurable in ω. For
each fixed ω, the source formulas show that the map is continuous in Z. The Carath´eodory
measurability criterion on a separable metric domain therefore makes (ω, Z) 7→PΞ(ω),yZ
jointly Borel measurable. Lemma 5.7 applies.
□

out any additional fixed point in ZT . The perturbative estimate (5.51) and absorption of the
first term give local Lipschitz dependence.

Propositions 5.15 and 5.29
For a fixed-profile dyadic family, the distributional components converge almost surely along
the full sequence and the operator components converge in the pathwise operator topologies
of Theorem 4.41. For an arbitrary admissible cofinal sequence, the distributional components
converge in every finite probability moment, the centered operator components converge in the
stated Lp(Ω) operator norms, and the resulting solution data converge in probability. Two
non-nested cutoff families have the same limits.
Proof. The displayed finite-cutoff formulas give the listed legs: (3.18), (3.22), (3.26), (B.14)–
(B.15), and the kernels in Sections 4.1–4. Applying the finite-product identity in Lemma 2.16
to each formula gives the asserted support property, including repeated legs with multiplicity.
The cited tail estimates are uniform over admissible cutoff profiles and are summable in their
respective path or operator norms.
The forcing identity (2.26) preserves the stochastic frequency support of its displayed
factors. The finite-cutoff source formulas (3.15)– (3.16) are assembled componentwise, so
their convergence follows from the corresponding tails and the continuity of the source maps.
Fixed-profile dyadic almost-sure convergence follows from the polynomial tail bounds and
the finite-state stabilization of fixed blocks. For two arbitrary cutoff families, (2.43) bounds
the difference by the sum of the two tails relative to the uncut object, proving the final
assertion.
□

Theorem 5.33 (Localized solutions and independence of localization). For each adapted
localization pair and each fixed-profile dyadic cutoff family, there are an event of probability one
and a strictly positive random time such that the finite-cutoff fixed points, reconstructed fields,
and nonlinear sources converge along the full dyadic sequence. For an arbitrary admissible
cofinal sequence, the corresponding convergence holds locally in probability on the deterministic
contraction events (5.68); these events exhaust probability one as in (5.73).
If two localization pairs are adapted to the same compact cone, their limiting fields and
sources agree on the overlap up to the minimum existence time. For every compact K and
every T below the two existence times, the common restriction to [0, T] × K satisfies (2.3) in
the sense of Definition 2.30. It is independent of the localization pair and of the admissible
spectral cutoff profile.

Lemma A.12 (Exhaustion of the three-frequency configurations). Assume N0 = ⟨ξ⟩, N1 =
⟨η⟩, and N2 = ⟨ξ −η⟩are all at least two. After enlarging the fixed comparability constants,
the dyadic frequency space is the union of the following four regions, with only finite overlap:
N0 ∼N1 ∼N2,
N0 ∼N2 ≫N1,
N0 ∼N1 ≫N2,
N1 ∼N2 ≫N0.
In the first three regions the sublevel estimates of Lemma A.10 apply to every sign vector
ε ∈{±1}3. In the last region the pointwise estimate of Lemma A.11 applies to every sign
vector.
Proof. The triangle inequalities imply that the two largest numbers among N0, N1, N2 are
comparable. If the smallest one is comparable to the other two, the first region occurs. If it is
strictly smaller, it is one of N0, N1, N2, giving respectively the fourth, second, or third region.
The derivative estimates in Lemma A.10 are estimates for absolute values and are therefore
independent of the signs. In the low-output region, equal signs on the two input phases give
their sum, while opposite signs give their difference; these are the two cases in the proof of
Lemma A.11.
□

Appendix B. Cubic resonant products

Γa = Va⊥◦Ψa.

The colored Wick expansion has a centered third-chaos component and one first-chaos

contraction is represented by an integrated Volterra kernel. Both branches use phase functions
determined solely by the wave and Klein–Gordon dispersions. Their amplitude order is

These are all the projection factors in the finite-cutoff algebra.
The algebraic formula below is conveniently written in the non-conjugated Fourier covariance
convention (2.39), whereas covariance densities are computed with the conjugated random-
measure convention (A.13). These are two presentations of the same real isonormal processes;
the Hermitian reality relation identifies the opposite-frequency coordinates. The passage to
momentum fibers below is made only after complexifying the real Gaussian Hilbert spaces;
the complexification is the full complex Fourier L2 space and therefore admits the usual
direct-integral decomposition.

Lemma B.2 (Finite-cutoff cubic Wick formula). For every cutoff and every pair of projection
flags, (B.6), (B.19), and (B.20) are identities in L2(Ω; S′(R3)) at each fixed time. The only
nonzero contraction pairs the two color-a legs at frequencies η and r. It imposes
η + r = 0,

is given by (B.15).
Proof. At finite cutoff all deterministic Wiener kernels are square integrable and have compact
spatial-frequency support. Apply the colored product formula (B.16) to the two-noise kernel
defining Va⊥,Λ and the color-a kernel defining Ψa,Λ. Orthogonality of the two Gaussian color
spaces excludes all cross-color contractions. The single same-color contraction is evaluated
with (B.17); its delta distribution forces r = −η, and the momentum constraint η + ζ + r = ξ
then gives ζ = ξ. Substitution in (B.14) yields (B.15). The multiple Wiener isometry and
deterministic Fubini theorem justify the identities in L2(Ω; S′).
□

with polynomial decay.
The cutoff multipliers in (B.15) form a finite product with the repeated r-leg listed twice.
Hence Lemma 2.16 gives the same tail for all four projection conventions and compares two
non-nested admissible cutoffs through the sum of their tails. This completes the proof.

Proof of Theorem B.1. The finite decomposition, the identity of the surviving contraction,
and the remaining random frequency are given by Lemma B.2. The centered third-chaos
convergence and regularity are Proposition B.5 and (B.35). The first-chaos contraction is first
defined at finite cutoff by (B.36); Proposition B.12 and (B.79) give its limiting construction.
The cutoff tail estimates apply to every family satisfying Definition 2.15. In particular,
inserting one of the auxiliary output projections in (B.5) does not change the limit.
It remains to verify the source topology. Fix an exponent quadruple in (2.48). Choose
